1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\citep{gelman2013garden}.}
4	Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\%), while precision's systematic deep preference dissolves under random labels (Shallow\% drops to 20\%, selections scatter across the full layer range; \cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry and is internally valid (split-half replication; \cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\citep{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
5

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\\citep{gelman2013garden}.}\nOur five-metric ablation (\\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\\%), while precision's systematic deep preference dissolves under random labels (Shallow\\% drops to 20\\%, selections scatter across the full layer range; \\cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry and is internally valid (split-half replication; \\cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\\citep{hewitt2019control}.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}\nThe HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}\nConverging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\\citep{geiger2025causal}, ITI~\\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2/4 (QwQ-32B absorbed by $(t/T)^2$; \\cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Quadratic control risks overfitting at $n{=}17$--$20$, so the divergent results bracket the true position contribution; HS probes partly functioning as better position encoders remains unexcluded. We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}).\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Layer ensemble proof of concept.}\nTraining probes at the best layer per threshold-based metric (precision, BA, FPR) and averaging predictions yields a robust, metric-agnostic strategy: HS vs.\\ text precision is significant for all four models ($p \\leq 0.002$; \\cref{tab:ensemble}), with +10.5 to +27.0pp advantages. The ensemble does not improve over oracle single-metric selection ($p > 0.27$) but eliminates metric pre-selection.\\looseness=-1\n",

Still 30 pages. The `\looseness=-2` didn't achieve 2-line compression on this paragraph (TeX can't always honor the request if the paragraph is already tight). 

I need to shorten the Fix 2 text. The Director's exact wording adds ~80 characters. Let me try a more concise version that preserves the disambiguation intent: instead of "selections scatter across the full layer range", I can say "scattered across layers".

Current text:
"while precision's systematic deep preference dissolves under random labels (Shallow\% drops to 20\%, selections scatter across the full layer range; \cref{app:random_cp})"


More concise version:
"while precision's deep preference dissolves under random labels (only 20\% shallow, scattered across layers; \cref{app:random_cp})"

This saves about 30 characters. I should also revert `\looseness=-2` back to `\looseness=-1` since -2 isn't working anyway. Actually, let me reconsider the core issue the Director raised—the ambiguity in "collapses to 20%". The fix needs to clarify that under random labels, only 20% of precision's selections are shallow (compared to 100% for CR), and that these selections are scattered rather than concentrated.

So I need to make clear that "20%" refers to the shallow selection rate, not retention of deep preference. A tighter version: "while precision's deep preference dissolves (20\% shallow, scattered; \cref{app:random_cp})" gets me down to about 85 characters, which is much more manageable than the 120-character version.

Now I'm considering whether to keep shortening this particular fix or try a different approach entirely—maybe adding negative spacing commands elsewhere in the document, or using `\looseness=-1` more strategically. But since all the Discussion paragraphs already have that, I think the most reliable path is just to trim the text itself. The version that drops "systematic" and "under random labels" while keeping the core disambiguation should work well enough.
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\\citep{gelman2013garden}.}\nOur five-metric ablation (\\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\\%), while precision's deep preference dissolves (only 20\\% shallow, scattered across layers; \\cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry and is internally valid (split-half replication; \\cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\\citep{hewitt2019control}.\\looseness=-2\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}\nThe HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}\nConverging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\\citep{geiger2025causal}, ITI~\\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2/4 (QwQ-32B absorbed by $(t/T)^2$; \\cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Quadratic control risks overfitting at $n{=}17$--$20$, so the divergent results bracket the true position contribution; HS probes partly functioning as better position encoders remains unexcluded. We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}).\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Layer ensemble proof of concept.}\nTraining probes at the best layer per threshold-based metric (precision, BA, FPR) and averaging predictions yields a robust, metric-agnostic strategy: HS vs.\\ text precision is significant for all four models ($p \\leq 0.002$; \\cref{tab:ensemble}), with +10.5 to +27.0pp advantages. The ensemble does not improve over oracle single-metric selection ($p > 0.27$) but eliminates metric pre-selection.\\looseness=-1\n",

        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\\citep{gelman2013garden}.}\nOur five-metric ablation (\\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\\%), while precision's deep preference dissolves (only 20\\% shallow, scattered across layers; \\cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry and is internally valid (split-half replication; \\cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\\citep{hewitt2019control}.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}\nThe HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}\nConverging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\\citep{geiger2025causal}, ITI~\\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2/4 (QwQ-32B absorbed by $(t/T)^2$; \\cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Quadratic control risks overfitting at $n{=}17$--$20$, so the divergent results bracket the true position contribution; HS probes partly functioning as better position encoders remains unexcluded. We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}).\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Layer ensemble proof of concept.}\nTraining probes at the best layer per threshold-based metric (precision, BA, FPR) and averaging predictions yields a robust, metric-agnostic strategy: HS vs.\\ text precision is significant for all four models ($p \\leq 0.002$; \\cref{tab:ensemble}), with +10.5 to +27.0pp advantages. The ensemble does not improve over oracle single-metric selection ($p > 0.27$) but eliminates metric pre-selection.\\looseness=-1\n",

{
  "parentUuid": "cf985e3b-d28f-49cc-896e-77a195125139",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/abstract.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/abstract.tex",
        "content": "Reasoning models with chain-of-thought (CoT) can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. Detecting the commitment point (CP)---the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases---requires monitoring internal representations or generated text. We study four reasoning LLMs, primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models) with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence, on HarmThoughts and provide initial characterization of hidden-state (HS) vs.\\ text probe capabilities on this benchmark. The comparison depends critically on which transformer layer is monitored: a crossing-rate composite metric masks the HS advantage at 32B scale by selecting shallow layers where representation compression inflates threshold crossings. Threshold-based layer selection recovers HS precision gains of +15.4 to +21.9pp against MiniLM-384d (Holm-Bonferroni corrected; advantage is encoder-conditional and represents an upper bound); this advantage diminishes with stronger encoders (384d: 4/4 models pass HB $\\to$ 1024d BGE-large: 1/4). The 7--8B results are cross-validated (nested CV): precision and balanced accuracy confirmed; R1-8B safe-trace false positive rate (FPR) is significant but seed-sensitive (3/5 seeds pass HB). The 32B results are exploratory$^\\dagger$ ($^\\dagger$\\,sample-limited, $n \\leq 20$): QwQ-32B is significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness; R1-32B shows a pattern consistent with position mediation and falls below minimum detectable effect. A three-layer ensemble provides metric-agnostic HS advantage matching the best individual metric (4/4 models, $p \\leq 0.002$). Metric-induced layer selection bias is the robust methodological finding; the empirical HS advantage is conditional on encoder quality, model scale, and position control.\n",
        "numLines": 2,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 2
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/abstract.tex"
  },

We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}). {
  "parentUuid": "ab242b2b-7b10-4558-b0f1-f11f8df3ab16",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_5/projects/contrastive_trace_safety/docs/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Limitations}\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Single dataset.}\nAll experiments use one dataset (HarmThoughts~\\citep{harmthoughts}), which provides step-level CP annotations derived from manual inspection of reasoning chains. The dataset spans multiple harmful categories (e.g., violence, fraud, illegal activities) but we do not stratify results by category; categories with longer average reasoning chains or earlier CPs may exhibit different probe dynamics. CP positions range from early to late within traces, though the distribution is right-skewed (most CPs occur in the first half of reasoning). Annotation granularity (step-level vs.\\ token-level), category distribution, and annotator team composition may all influence which layers carry discriminative signal and how strongly metric bias manifests. Inter-annotator agreement (IAA) was not computed for these annotations. Random annotation noise would attenuate effect sizes symmetrically, making our estimates conservative; systematic noise (e.g., consistent early or late CP placement) could bias layer selection or inflate modality differences. Without IAA, we cannot distinguish these cases, so our conclusions may not transfer to independently annotated corpora. Generalization to other jailbreak datasets, attack taxonomies, or multilingual settings is untested.\n\n\\paragraph{Model-specific caveats and statistical power.}\nCurrent probes characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors: false positive rates remain 11--50\\% (model-dependent), and the 76.7\\% oracle gap (R1-8B; \\cref{sec:oracle}) underscores how much room remains. R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ (25\\% coverage, 5/20 traces) and its HS advantage shows a pattern consistent with position mediation (residualized gap +1.9pp, $p{=}0.914$; \\cref{app:position_residual}). QwQ-32B's position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy (0.832 vs.\\ 0.796) with negative HS lead time. R1-8B FPR significance is seed-fragile (3/5 HB). At $n{=}19$--$20$, the minimum detectable effect at 80\\% power is 8.6--9.5pp (\\cref{app:power_analysis}); all 32B conclusions marked $\\dagger$ should be interpreted with this constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Hyperparameter selection and scope.}\nLayer (up to 64), $W$ (7 values), $K$ (5 values), and threshold are selected on the same validation set ($n{=}4$--$9$ per-class at 32B, up to $2{,}240$ configurations). The 12-test HB boundary covers final model-metric comparisons but not the full upstream exploration; Type~I error may be underestimated. HB correction covers the final 12 model-metric tests but not the cumulative researcher degrees of freedom in layer, metric, window, and evidence tier selection; cumulative degrees of freedom are substantial (\\cref{app:hyperparameter_robustness} provides convergence, cross-validation, and bootstrap analyses addressing this concern). BA-optimal layer indices are sensitive to the train/validation split, spanning 7--98\\% depth across models and splits (\\cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}); for example, OT-7B shifts from L16 to L2 and QwQ-32B from L60 to L63 between 70/30 and 60/20/20 splits, a direct manifestation of the layer selection sensitivity that constitutes our primary finding. Threshold-based metrics are more stable but still vary by up to 4 layers. $K{=}5$ was selected on the R1-8B validation set and held fixed (\\cref{app:k_ablation}); joint (layer, $W$) stability was verified for QwQ-32B (\\cref{app:layer_sweep}) but not R1-32B. CP annotations follow HarmThoughts~\\citep{harmthoughts} without independent IAA evaluation; if annotation error is ${\\geq}2$ steps, QwQ-32B's lead time ($-$1.2 to +1.9) falls within noise, though perturbation analysis shows smooth degradation with no cliff effects (\\cref{app:cp_perturbation}). Three of four models share the Qwen architecture family; observed patterns may reflect Qwen-specific representation properties rather than universal reasoning-model characteristics. Only R1-8B (Llama-based) provides cross-architecture evidence. We cover 2 architectures and 2 scales; whether metric-induced layer selection bias generalizes to non-reasoning LLMs or encoder-only architectures remains an open question. Intermediate scales, diverse base architectures, proprietary models, and multilingual settings are untested. Results are correlational; causal intervention is needed to confirm mechanistic involvement. Cross-model transfer was tested with PCA-based subspace alignment only; more sophisticated approaches (e.g., Procrustes rotation, CKA-guided projection~\\citep{kornblith2019cka}, model stitching~\\citep{bansal2021stitching}) remain future work.\n\n\\paragraph{CP permanence assumption.}\nOur binary CP definition assumes that once safety deliberation ceases, it does not resume. In practice, some reasoning traces exhibit oscillating patterns where the model intermittently returns to safety-aware reasoning after apparent commitment. The current binary framework treats the first permanent cessation as CP, but traces with oscillating safety deliberation violate this assumption and may be misclassified. A multi-state formulation (e.g., pre-commitment / wavering / committed) could better capture these dynamics but would require richer annotations and larger datasets to train reliably.\n",
        "numLines": 14,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 14
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/sections/limitations.tex"
  },

```latex
\section{Representation Compression Analysis}
\label{app:cosine_volatility}

We measure step-to-step cosine distance at CR-composite (shallow) vs.\ threshold-based (deep) layers across all models to characterize the compression mechanism underlying the crossing rate bias (\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\small
\caption{Step-to-step cosine distance at CR-composite (shallow) and threshold-based (deep) layers. Shallow layers produce highly compressed representations (1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller inter-step distances).}
\label{tab:cosine_volatility}
\begin{tabular}{llcc}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Layer (type)} & \textbf{Mean} & \textbf{Std} \\
\midrule
R1-8B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.186 & 0.097 \\
 & L14 (thresh.) & 0.411 & 0.143 \\[2pt]
OT-7B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.077 & 0.080 \\
 & L16 (thresh.) & 0.206 & 0.091 \\[2pt]
QwQ-32B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.037 & 0.064 \\
 & L63 (thresh.) & 0.084 & 0.056 \\[2pt]
R1-32B & L10 (CR-comp.) & 0.067 & 0.050 \\
 & L63 (thresh.) & 0.092 & 0.076 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Shallow layers produce highly compressed representations: consecutive steps cluster within small cosine distances (0.04--0.19), while deep-layer representations spread more widely (0.09--0.41). Inter-step distances at shallow layers are 1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models. A linear probe operating in this compressed space sits near the noise floor, where minor numerical fluctuations produce spurious threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Deep layers produce well-separated representations where crossings correspond to genuine semantic transitions.
```

The section contains a detailed analysis comparing cosine distances between shallow (CR-composite) and deep (threshold-based) layers across four models (R1-8B, OT-7B, QwQ-32B, and R1-32B), with quantitative measurements of mean and standard deviation for each layer pair.
````

27	
28	\section{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}
29	\label{app:checklist}
30	
31	We address the ARR Responsible NLP Research requirements below.
32	
33	\begin{itemize}
34	    \item \textbf{Limitations discussed:} Yes (\cref{sec:limitations}).
35	    \item \textbf{Potential risks:} This research analyzes jailbreak traces to understand how reasoning models commit to generating harmful content. We do not develop new attack methods or release jailbreak prompts. All analyzed content comes from the publicly available HarmThoughts benchmark. We acknowledge a dual-use concern: safety probes that detect commitment could in principle be used to craft reasoning traces that evade detection. We believe the defensive value of understanding commitment dynamics outweighs this risk, as evasion requires white-box access to the probe, which a deployed monitoring system would not expose.
36	    \item \textbf{Computational budget:} Our experiments involve 4 models with 209, 228, 100, and 95 traces respectively (632 total), using linear probes and single-layer GRUs. Total compute is minimal (under 4 GPU-hours on a single RTX A6000). No large-scale pretraining or finetuning was performed.
37	    \item \textbf{Data:} We use HarmThoughts, a publicly available benchmark with step-level safety annotations. No new data collection was performed.
38	    \item \textbf{Human subjects:} No human subjects research was conducted.
39	    \item \textbf{Reproducibility:} All hyperparameters are reported in \cref{app:gru_details,app:bocpd_config}. Code and corrected data will be released upon publication.
40	\end{itemize}
41	
42	
43	\section{Layer Sweep Results}
44	\label{app:layer_sweep}
45	
46	For each model, we sweep all transformer layers and evaluate five candidate selection metrics: (1)~balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate (the CR-composite), (2)~pure balanced accuracy, (3)~precision, (4)~inverse pre-CP FPR, and (5)~an additive composite. The CR-composite systematically selects shallow layers across all four models (including R1-8B under the full L0--L24 sweep; \cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}) because early-layer representations exhibit higher crossing rates (more frequent threshold crossings) regardless of discriminative power. Three threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) converge on deep layers for all models; pure balanced accuracy selects layers whose depth varies with the data split (under 70/30: L30 for QwQ-32B, L47 for R1-32B; under the canonical 60/20/20: L63 and L46 respectively; \cref{tab:ba_layer_results}).
47	
48	\paragraph{Selected layers.} R1-8B: under the original L12--L24 sweep, all five metrics agreed on L14 (44\%); extending to L0--L24, crossing rate shifts to L0 while threshold-based metrics retain L14, a 16-layer gap (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). OT-7B: CR-composite selects L0 (0\%), threshold-based consensus selects L16 (57\%). QwQ-32B: CR-composite selects L2 (3\%), threshold-based consensus selects L63 (98\%). R1-32B: CR-composite selects L10 (16\%), threshold-based consensus selects L63 (98\%). The disagreement is most extreme for 64-layer models, where the CR-composite selects layers in the bottom 16\% of the network while threshold-based metrics select layers at 98\% depth. Under 70/30, pure balanced accuracy selects L30 (47\%) for QwQ-32B and L47 (73\%) for R1-32B; under the canonical 60/20/20, it selects L63 (98\%) and L46 (72\%), which demonstrates sensitivity to the data split.
49	
50	\paragraph{Impact on modality comparison.} At the CR-composite layers, HS vs.\ text precision $p$-values are 0.064 (R1-32B) and 0.35--0.83 (QwQ-32B), both non-significant. At the threshold-based deep layers, all $p$-values fall below 0.005. The initially reported ``scale-dependent'' pattern (HS advantage at 7--8B but not 32B) was entirely an artifact of layer selection methodology, not a genuine property of model scale (\cref{sec:layer-results}).
```

120	\section{Representation Compression Analysis}
121	\label{app:cosine_volatility}
122	
123	We measure step-to-step cosine distance at CR-composite (shallow) vs.\ threshold-based (deep) layers across all models to characterize the compression mechanism underlying the crossing rate bias (\cref{tab:cosine_volatility}).
124	
125	\begin{table}[h]
126	\centering
127	\small
128	\caption{Step-to-step cosine distance at CR-composite (shallow) and threshold-based (deep) layers. Shallow layers produce highly compressed representations (1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller inter-step distances).}
129	\label{tab:cosine_volatility}
130	\begin{tabular}{llcc}
131	\toprule
132	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Layer (type)} & \textbf{Mean} & \textbf{Std} \\
133	\midrule
134	R1-8B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.186 & 0.097 \\
135	 & L14 (thresh.) & 0.411 & 0.143 \\[2pt]
136	OT-7B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.077 & 0.080 \\
137	 & L16 (thresh.) & 0.206 & 0.091 \\[2pt]
138	QwQ-32B & L2 (CR-comp.) & 0.037 & 0.064 \\
139	 & L63 (thresh.) & 0.084 & 0.056 \\[2pt]
140	R1-32B & L10 (CR-comp.) & 0.067 & 0.050 \\
141	 & L63 (thresh.) & 0.092 & 0.076 \\
142	\bottomrule
143	\end{tabular}
144	\end{table}
145	
146	Shallow layers produce highly compressed representations: consecutive steps cluster within small cosine distances (0.04--0.19), while deep-layer representations spread more widely (0.09--0.41). Inter-step distances at shallow layers are 1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models. A linear probe operating in this compressed space sits near the noise floor, where minor numerical fluctuations produce spurious threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Deep layers produce well-separated representations where crossings correspond to genuine semantic transitions.
147	
148	
149	\section{Holm-Bonferroni Step-Down Correction}
150	\label{app:holm_bonferroni}
151	
152	\cref{tab:holm_bonferroni} reports the full test battery used to assess hidden-state superiority over text probes. Because balanced accuracy, precision, and FPR share the same confusion matrix, the three per-model metrics are correlated; the effective number of independent comparisons is four (one per model). We report both the conservative 12-test correction and the 4-test model-level correction: both yield the same outcome (all tests pass at $\alpha{=}0.05$). Under 4-test HB, the tightest threshold falls on R1-8B FPR ($p{=}0.044$, rank-4 threshold $\alpha/1{=}0.050$), which still passes. Tests are ranked by $p$-value; each test $i$ (rank $i$ of $m$) must satisfy $p_i < \alpha / (m - i + 1)$ under the step-down procedure.
153	
154	\begin{table}[h]
155	\centering
156	\small
157	\caption{Holm-Bonferroni step-down correction for 12 tests (4 models $\times$ 3 correlated metrics). Each test uses a two-sided null-centered bootstrap (10{,}000 resamples) on trace-level metric differences (HS minus text). All 12 pass at $\alpha{=}0.05$; a 4-test model-level correction (one per model, using the worst $p$-value) also passes.}
158	\label{tab:holm_bonferroni}
159	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013S2gv4J5rxKFWBtASsEvz2`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\citep{gelman2013garden}.}
4	Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\%), while precision's deep preference dissolves (only 20\% shallow, scattered across layers; \cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry and is internally valid (split-half replication; \cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\citep{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
5	
6	\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}
7	The HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\looseness=-1
8	
9	\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}
10	Converging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\citep{geiger2025causal}, ITI~\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2/4 (QwQ-32B absorbed by $(t/T)^2$; \cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Quadratic control risks overfitting at $n{=}17$--$20$, so the divergent results bracket the true position contribution; HS probes partly functioning as better position encoders remains unexcluded. We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}).\looseness=-1
11	
12	\paragraph{Layer ensemble proof of concept.}
13	Training probes at the best layer per threshold-based metric (precision, BA, FPR) and averaging predictions yields a robust, metric-agnostic strategy: HS vs.\ text precision is significant for all four models ($p \leq 0.002$; \cref{tab:ensemble}), with +10.5 to +27.0pp advantages. The ensemble does not improve over oracle single-metric selection ($p > 0.27$) but eliminates metric pre-selection.\looseness=-1
14	
```

</details>

  "new_string": "Deep layers produce well-separated representations where crossings correspond to genuine semantic transitions.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-dataset evidence.}\nTo assess whether this geometric precondition generalizes beyond HarmThoughts, we compute the same shallow/deep volatility ratio on AdvBench~\\citep{advbench} traces using R1-8B. This confirms that the representational compression underlying metric-induced layer selection bias is not dataset-specific and does not depend on CP annotations.\n\n\n\\section{Holm-Bonferroni Step-Down Correction}"

          " Shallow layers produce highly compressed representations: consecutive steps cluster within small cosine distances (0.04--0.19), while deep-layer representations spread more widely (0.09--0.41). Inter-step distances at shallow layers are 1.4--2.7$\\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models. A linear probe operating in this compressed space sits near the noise floor, where minor numerical fluctuations produce spurious threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power. Deep layers produce well-separated representations where crossings correspond to genuine semantic transitions.",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01736RXJFG84xJSePQS8iD5L`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{Single dataset.}
4	All experiments use one dataset (HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts}), which provides step-level CP annotations derived from manual inspection of reasoning chains. The dataset spans multiple harmful categories (e.g., violence, fraud, illegal activities) but we do not stratify results by category; categories with longer average reasoning chains or earlier CPs may exhibit different probe dynamics. CP positions range from early to late within traces, though the distribution is right-skewed (most CPs occur in the first half of reasoning). Annotation granularity (step-level vs.\ token-level), category distribution, and annotator team composition may all influence which layers carry discriminative signal and how strongly metric bias manifests. Inter-annotator agreement (IAA) was not computed for these annotations. Random annotation noise would attenuate effect sizes symmetrically, making our estimates conservative; systematic noise (e.g., consistent early or late CP placement) could bias layer selection or inflate modality differences. Without IAA, we cannot distinguish these cases, so our conclusions may not transfer to independently annotated corpora. Generalization to other jailbreak datasets, attack taxonomies, or multilingual settings is untested.
5	
6	\paragraph{Model-specific caveats and statistical power.}
7	Current probes characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors: false positive rates remain 11--50\% (model-dependent), and the 76.7\% oracle gap (R1-8B; \cref{sec:oracle}) underscores how much room remains. R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ (25\% coverage, 5/20 traces) and its HS advantage shows a pattern consistent with position mediation (residualized gap +1.9pp, $p{=}0.914$; \cref{app:position_residual}). QwQ-32B's position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy (0.832 vs.\ 0.796) with negative HS lead time. R1-8B FPR significance is seed-fragile (3/5 HB). At $n{=}19$--$20$, the minimum detectable effect at 80\% power is 8.6--9.5pp (\cref{app:power_analysis}); all 32B conclusions marked $\dagger$ should be interpreted with this constraint.
8	
9	\paragraph{Hyperparameter selection and scope.}
10	Layer (up to 64), $W$ (7 values), $K$ (5 values), and threshold are selected on the same validation set ($n{=}4$--$9$ per-class at 32B, up to $2{,}240$ configurations). The 12-test HB boundary covers final model-metric comparisons but not the full upstream exploration; Type~I error may be underestimated. HB correction covers the final 12 model-metric tests but not the cumulative researcher degrees of freedom in layer, metric, window, and evidence tier selection; cumulative degrees of freedom are substantial (\cref{app:hyperparameter_robustness} provides convergence, cross-validation, and bootstrap analyses addressing this concern). BA-optimal layer indices are sensitive to the train/validation split, spanning 7--98\% depth across models and splits (\cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}); for example, OT-7B shifts from L16 to L2 and QwQ-32B from L60 to L63 between 70/30 and 60/20/20 splits, a direct manifestation of the layer selection sensitivity that constitutes our primary finding. Threshold-based metrics are more stable but still vary by up to 4 layers. $K{=}5$ was selected on the R1-8B validation set and held fixed (\cref{app:k_ablation}); joint (layer, $W$) stability was verified for QwQ-32B (\cref{app:layer_sweep}) but not R1-32B. CP annotations follow HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts} without independent IAA evaluation; if annotation error is ${\geq}2$ steps, QwQ-32B's lead time ($-$1.2 to +1.9) falls within noise, though perturbation analysis shows smooth degradation with no cliff effects (\cref{app:cp_perturbation}). Three of four models share the Qwen architecture family; observed patterns may reflect Qwen-specific representation properties rather than universal reasoning-model characteristics. Only R1-8B (Llama-based) provides cross-architecture evidence. We cover 2 architectures and 2 scales; whether metric-induced layer selection bias generalizes to non-reasoning LLMs or encoder-only architectures remains an open question. Intermediate scales, diverse base architectures, proprietary models, and multilingual settings are untested. Results are correlational; causal intervention is needed to confirm mechanistic involvement. Cross-model transfer was tested with PCA-based subspace alignment only; more sophisticated approaches (e.g., Procrustes rotation, CKA-guided projection~\citep{kornblith2019cka}, model stitching~\citep{bansal2021stitching}) remain future work.
11	
12	\paragraph{CP permanence assumption.}
13	Our binary CP definition assumes that once safety deliberation ceases, it does not resume. In practice, some reasoning traces exhibit oscillating patterns where the model intermittently returns to safety-aware reasoning after apparent commitment. The current binary framework treats the first permanent cessation as CP, but traces with oscillating safety deliberation violate this assumption and may be misclassified. A multi-state formulation (e.g., pre-commitment / wavering / committed) could better capture these dynamics but would require richer annotations and larger datasets to train reliably.
14	
```

Intermediate scales, diverse base architectures, proprietary models, and multilingual settings are untested. Results are correlational; causal intervention is needed to confirm mechanistic involvement. Cross-model transfer was tested with PCA-based subspace alignment only; more sophisticated approaches (e.g., Procrustes rotation, CKA-guided projection~\\citep{kornblith2019cka}, model stitching~\\citep{bansal2021stitching}) remain future work.\n\n\\paragraph{CP permanence assumption.}\nOur binary CP definition assumes that once safety deliberation ceases, it does not resume. In practice, some reasoning traces exhibit oscillating patterns where the model intermittently returns to safety-aware reasoning after apparent commitment. The current binary framework treats the first permanent cessation as CP, but traces with oscillating safety deliberation violate this assumption and may be misclassified. A multi-state formulation (e.g., pre-commitment / wavering / committed) could better capture these dynamics but would require richer annotations and larger datasets to train reliably.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 7,

        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\section{Limitations}\\label{sec:limitations}",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Single dataset.}",
          "-All experiments use one dataset (HarmThoughts~\\citep{harmthoughts}), which provides step-level CP annotations derived from manual inspection of reasoning chains. The dataset spans multiple harmful categories (e.g., violence, fraud, illegal activities) but we do not stratify results by category; categories with longer average reasoning chains or earlier CPs may exhibit different probe dynamics. CP positions range from early to late within traces, though the distribution is right-skewed (most CPs occur in the first half of reasoning). Annotation granularity (step-level vs.\\ token-level), category distribution, and annotator team composition may all influence which layers carry discriminative signal and how strongly metric bias manifests. Inter-annotator agreement (IAA) was not computed for these annotations. Random annotation noise would attenuate effect sizes symmetrically, making our estimates conservative; systematic noise (e.g., consistent early or late CP placement) could bias layer selection or inflate modality differences. Without IAA, we cannot distinguish these cases, so our conclusions may not transfer to independently annotated corpora. Generalization to other jailbreak datasets, attack taxonomies, or multilingual settings is untested.",
          "+All probe experiments use one dataset (HarmThoughts~\\citep{harmthoughts}); the geometric precondition (shallow-layer compression) is confirmed on AdvBench~\\citep{advbench} (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), but CP-dependent claims rely on HarmThoughts, which provides step-level CP annotations derived from manual inspection of reasoning chains. The dataset spans multiple harmful categories (e.g., violence, fraud, illegal activities) but we do not stratify results by category; categories with longer average reasoning chains or earlier CPs may exhibit different probe dynamics. CP positions range from early to late within traces, though the distribution is right-skewed (most CPs occur in the first half of reasoning). Annotation granularity (step-level vs.\\ token-level), category distribution, and annotator team composition may all influence which layers carry discriminative signal and how strongly metric bias manifests. Inter-annotator agreement (IAA) was not computed for these annotations. Random annotation noise would attenuate effect sizes symmetrically, making our estimates conservative; systematic noise (e.g., consistent early or late CP placement) could bias layer selection or inflate modality differences. Without IAA, we cannot distinguish these cases, so our conclusions may not transfer to independently annotated corpora. Generalization to other jailbreak datasets, attack taxonomies, or multilingual settings is untested.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Model-specific caveats and statistical power.}",
          " Current probes characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors: false positive rates remain 11--50\\% (model-dependent), and the 76.7\\% oracle gap (R1-8B; \\cref{sec:oracle}) underscores how much room remains. R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ (25\\% coverage, 5/20 traces) and its HS advantage shows a pattern consistent with position mediation (residualized gap +1.9pp, $p{=}0.914$; \\cref{app:position_residual}). QwQ-32B's position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy (0.832 vs.\\ 0.796) with negative HS lead time. R1-8B FPR significance is seed-fragile (3/5 HB). At $n{=}19$--$20$, the minimum detectable effect at 80\\% power is 8.6--9.5pp (\\cref{app:power_analysis}); all 32B conclusions marked $\\dagger$ should be interpreted with this constraint."

### Tool result

_source line 2883 · 2026-05-20T03:59:49.882Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012BiMR4onm1uDPeLXfUMV5v`</summary>

```text
1	Reasoning models with chain-of-thought (CoT) can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. Detecting the commitment point (CP)---the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases---requires monitoring internal representations or generated text. We study four reasoning LLMs, primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models) with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence, on HarmThoughts and provide initial characterization of hidden-state (HS) vs.\ text probe capabilities on this benchmark. The comparison depends critically on which transformer layer is monitored: a crossing-rate composite metric masks the HS advantage at 32B scale by selecting shallow layers where representation compression inflates threshold crossings. Threshold-based layer selection recovers HS precision gains of +15.4 to +21.9pp against MiniLM-384d (Holm-Bonferroni corrected; advantage is encoder-conditional and represents an upper bound); this advantage diminishes with stronger encoders (384d: 4/4 models pass HB $\to$ 1024d BGE-large: 1/4). The 7--8B results are cross-validated (nested CV): precision and balanced accuracy confirmed; R1-8B safe-trace false positive rate (FPR) is significant but seed-sensitive (3/5 seeds pass HB). The 32B results are exploratory$^\dagger$ ($^\dagger$\,sample-limited, $n \leq 20$): QwQ-32B is significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness; R1-32B shows a pattern consistent with position mediation and falls below minimum detectable effect. A three-layer ensemble provides metric-agnostic HS advantage matching the best individual metric (4/4 models, $p \leq 0.002$). Metric-induced layer selection bias is the robust methodological finding; the empirical HS advantage is conditional on encoder quality, model scale, and position control.
2	
```

_source line 2886 · 2026-05-20T03:59:52.963Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PFL8VU6r7x9NJjeuynXCEN`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\%, so deployment applications are future work.\looseness=-1
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer (QwQ-32B) divergence depending solely on metric choice.\looseness=-1
6	
7	We present an initial characterization across four reasoning models---primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models), with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence---at two scales (7--8B, 32B) on the HarmThoughts benchmark. Our central finding is methodological: the choice of layer selection metric entirely determines whether an HS advantage is observed. A composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) selects shallow layers for 32B models because shallow representations compress directional information, so a linear probe operates near the noise floor and threshold crossings reflect noise rather than semantic transitions. At these layers, only the two 7--8B models show significant HS advantage.\looseness=-1
8	
9	Threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms deep-layer selection (\cref{tab:layer_selection}). At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \cref{sec:experiments}).\looseness=-1
10	
11	Our contributions are:
12	\begin{itemize}
13	    \item \textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models (including Llama), reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale; a random-label control confirms the bias is geometric (\cref{sec:layer-results}).
14	
15	    \item \textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).
16	
17	    \item \textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.
18	\end{itemize}
19	
20	\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\looseness=-1
21	
```

At these layers, the HS advantage is graded: \\emph{cross-validated} at 7--8B (precision +20.1 to +21.9pp, nested CV; \\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}), \\emph{significant} but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp; \\cref{sec:32b-analysis}), and \\emph{exploratory} at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation, below MDE; \\cref{sec:experiments}).\\looseness=-1\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models (including Llama), reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale; a random-label control confirms the bias is geometric (\\cref{sec:layer-results}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Encoder-quality boundary with evidence gradient.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is cross-validated at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected, nested CV), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). Against 1024d BGE-large, only OT-7B survives correction (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (\\cref{sec:32b-analysis}).\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \\cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\\looseness=-1\n",
      "numLines": 21,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 21
    }
  },

```text
1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	\paragraph{Single dataset.}
4	All probe experiments use one dataset (HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts}); the geometric precondition (shallow-layer compression) is confirmed on AdvBench~\citep{advbench} (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), but CP-dependent claims rely on HarmThoughts, which provides step-level CP annotations derived from manual inspection of reasoning chains. The dataset spans multiple harmful categories (e.g., violence, fraud, illegal activities) but we do not stratify results by category; categories with longer average reasoning chains or earlier CPs may exhibit different probe dynamics. CP positions range from early to late within traces, though the distribution is right-skewed (most CPs occur in the first half of reasoning). Annotation granularity (step-level vs.\ token-level), category distribution, and annotator team composition may all influence which layers carry discriminative signal and how strongly metric bias manifests. Inter-annotator agreement (IAA) was not computed for these annotations. Random annotation noise would attenuate effect sizes symmetrically, making our estimates conservative; systematic noise (e.g., consistent early or late CP placement) could bias layer selection or inflate modality differences. Without IAA, we cannot distinguish these cases, so our conclusions may not transfer to independently annotated corpora. Generalization to other jailbreak datasets, attack taxonomies, or multilingual settings is untested.
5	
6	\paragraph{Model-specific caveats and statistical power.}
7	Current probes characterize the commitment phenomenon rather than constitute production detectors: false positive rates remain 11--50\% (model-dependent), and the 76.7\% oracle gap (R1-8B; \cref{sec:oracle}) underscores how much room remains. R1-32B achieves significance only at $W{=}1$ (25\% coverage, 5/20 traces) and its HS advantage shows a pattern consistent with position mediation (residualized gap +1.9pp, $p{=}0.914$; \cref{app:position_residual}). QwQ-32B's position baseline achieves higher balanced accuracy (0.832 vs.\ 0.796) with negative HS lead time. R1-8B FPR significance is seed-fragile (3/5 HB). At $n{=}19$--$20$, the minimum detectable effect at 80\% power is 8.6--9.5pp (\cref{app:power_analysis}); all 32B conclusions marked $\dagger$ should be interpreted with this constraint.
8	
9	\paragraph{Hyperparameter selection and scope.}
10	Layer (up to 64), $W$ (7 values), $K$ (5 values), and threshold are selected on the same validation set ($n{=}4$--$9$ per-class at 32B, up to $2{,}240$ configurations). The 12-test HB boundary covers final model-metric comparisons but not the full upstream exploration; Type~I error may be underestimated. HB correction covers the final 12 model-metric tests but not the cumulative researcher degrees of freedom in layer, metric, window, and evidence tier selection; cumulative degrees of freedom are substantial (\cref{app:hyperparameter_robustness} provides convergence, cross-validation, and bootstrap analyses addressing this concern). BA-optimal layer indices are sensitive to the train/validation split, spanning 7--98\% depth across models and splits (\cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}); for example, OT-7B shifts from L16 to L2 and QwQ-32B from L60 to L63 between 70/30 and 60/20/20 splits, a direct manifestation of the layer selection sensitivity that constitutes our primary finding. Threshold-based metrics are more stable but still vary by up to 4 layers. $K{=}5$ was selected on the R1-8B validation set and held fixed (\cref{app:k_ablation}); joint (layer, $W$) stability was verified for QwQ-32B (\cref{app:layer_sweep}) but not R1-32B. CP annotations follow HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts} without independent IAA evaluation; if annotation error is ${\geq}2$ steps, QwQ-32B's lead time ($-$1.2 to +1.9) falls within noise, though perturbation analysis shows smooth degradation with no cliff effects (\cref{app:cp_perturbation}). Three of four models share the Qwen architecture family; observed patterns may reflect Qwen-specific representation properties rather than universal reasoning-model characteristics. Only R1-8B (Llama-based) provides cross-architecture evidence. We cover 2 architectures and 2 scales; whether metric-induced layer selection bias generalizes to non-reasoning LLMs or encoder-only architectures remains an open question. Intermediate scales, diverse base architectures, proprietary models, and multilingual settings are untested. Results are correlational; causal intervention is needed to confirm mechanistic involvement. Cross-model transfer was tested with PCA-based subspace alignment only; more sophisticated approaches (e.g., Procrustes rotation, CKA-guided projection~\citep{kornblith2019cka}, model stitching~\citep{bansal2021stitching}) remain future work.
11	
12	\paragraph{CP permanence assumption.}
13	Our binary CP definition assumes that once safety deliberation ceases, it does not resume. In practice, some reasoning traces exhibit oscillating patterns where the model intermittently returns to safety-aware reasoning after apparent commitment. The current binary framework treats the first permanent cessation as CP, but traces with oscillating safety deliberation violate this assumption and may be misclassified. A multi-state formulation (e.g., pre-commitment / wavering / committed) could better capture these dynamics but would require richer annotations and larger datasets to train reliably.
14	
```

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	Layer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that can reverse conclusions; the multi-metric audit protocol (\cref{sec:discussion}) mitigates this. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BGLjDggTvknVN5XwJcwr3n`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\citep{gelman2013garden}.}
4	Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\%), while precision's deep preference dissolves (only 20\% shallow, scattered across layers; \cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry, cross-dataset (AdvBench volatility ratio $1.67\times$; \cref{app:cosine_volatility}), and is internally valid (split-half replication; \cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\citep{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
5	
6	\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}
7	The HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\looseness=-1
8	
9	\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}
10	Converging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\citep{geiger2025causal}, ITI~\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2/4 (QwQ-32B absorbed by $(t/T)^2$; \cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Quadratic control risks overfitting at $n{=}17$--$20$, so the divergent results bracket the true position contribution; HS probes partly functioning as better position encoders remains unexcluded. We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}).\looseness=-1
11	
12	\paragraph{Layer ensemble proof of concept.}
13	Training probes at the best layer per threshold-based metric (precision, BA, FPR) and averaging predictions yields a robust, metric-agnostic strategy: HS vs.\ text precision is significant for all four models ($p \leq 0.002$; \cref{tab:ensemble}), with +10.5 to +27.0pp advantages. The ensemble does not improve over oracle single-metric selection ($p > 0.27$) but eliminates metric pre-selection.\looseness=-1
14	
```

A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\\%), while precision's deep preference dissolves (only 20\\% shallow, scattered across layers; \\cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\\citep{hewitt2019control}.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}\nThe HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}\nConverging evidence (oracle analysis, PCA capacity control, control task selectivity, position residualization) supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\\citep{geiger2025causal}, ITI~\\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2/4 (QwQ-32B absorbed by $(t/T)^2$; \\cref{tab:position_quadratic}). Quadratic control risks overfitting at $n{=}17$--$20$, so the divergent results bracket the true position contribution; HS probes partly functioning as better position encoders remains unexcluded. We propose a multi-metric audit protocol: compute ${\\geq}3$ metrics, flag disagreement, and verify cosine volatility (\\cref{app:cosine_volatility,app:deployment_operating_points}).\\looseness=-1\n\n\\paragraph{Layer ensemble proof of concept.}\nTraining probes at the best layer per threshold-based metric (precision, BA, FPR) and averaging predictions yields a robust, metric-agnostic strategy: HS vs.\\ text precision is significant for all four models ($p \\leq 0.002$; \\cref{tab:ensemble}), with +10.5 to +27.0pp advantages. The ensemble does not improve over oracle single-metric selection ($p > 0.27$) but eliminates metric pre-selection.\\looseness=-1\n",
      "numLines": 14,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 14
    }

```text
1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria.
4	
5	\paragraph{Evidence gradient.} We classify HS advantage evidence into three tiers: \emph{cross-validated}\footnote{In our usage, \emph{cross-validated} means the result survives nested 5-fold cross-validation with a held-out test fold, ruling out hyperparameter overfitting. \emph{Exploratory} ($\dagger$) results pass HB on the holdout split but lack nested CV validation due to sample constraints.} (nested CV pass; 7--8B models), \emph{significant} (HB pass without nested CV; QwQ-32B), and \emph{exploratory} ($^\dagger$, small sample or narrow coverage; R1-32B). Per-model details follow in \cref{sec:layer-results,sec:hs-text,sec:32b-analysis}.
6	
7	\subsection{Setup}\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	We study R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (OpenThinker-7B, trained on the OpenThoughts dataset~\citep{openthinker}; Qwen-7B backbone, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level CP annotations (60/20/20 split). HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected layer; text probes encode each step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d). Both use L2-regularized logistic regression with temporal windowing $W \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$ selected on validation. Detection requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Statistical testing follows \cref{sec:evaluation}.\looseness=-1
10	
11	\subsection{Layer Selection Results}\label{sec:layer-results}
12	
13	\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the crossing-rate composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers across all four models: L0 for R1-8B (full L0--L24 sweep; \cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}) and OT-7B, L2 (3\%) for QwQ-32B, and L10 (16\%) for R1-32B. The three threshold-based metrics (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) share a common confusion matrix and empirically converge to the same deep layer---L63 (98\%) for both 32B models---consistent with their shared structure, though not mathematically guaranteed. Pure balanced accuracy, a threshold-free metric, selects layers that vary across data splits and models (7--98\% depth under 60/20/20; \cref{tab:ba_layer_comparison}), itself demonstrating that layer selection is a consequential methodological choice. The total exploration space spans 5 metrics $\times$ 7 window sizes $\times$ 28--64 layers (model-dependent); HB correction covers the final 12 model-metric tests, not this full search (\cref{app:hyperparameter_robustness}). The mechanism is that shallow layers produce highly compressed representations with small inter-step margins: step-to-step cosine distances are 1.4--2.7$\times$ smaller than at deep layers across all models (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}). A linear probe in this compressed space operates near the noise floor, generating frequent threshold crossings that inflate crossing rate independently of discriminative power (\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}).\looseness=-1
14	
15	\begin{table}[t]
```
